Reconciling Event Structures with Modern Multiprocessors
Evgenii Moiseenko, Anton Podkopaev, Ori Lahav, Orestis Melkonian, Viktor Vafeiadis

Abstract
𝖶𝖾𝖺𝗄𝖾𝗌𝗍𝗆𝗈 \mathsf{Weakestmo} is a recently proposed memory consistency model that uses event structures to resolve the infamous “out-of-thin-air” problem and to enable efficient compilation to hardware. Nevertheless, this latter property—compilation correctness—has not yet been formally established. This paper closes this gap by establishing correctness of the intended compilation schemes from 𝖶𝖾𝖺𝗄𝖾𝗌𝗍𝗆𝗈 \mathsf{Weakestmo} to a wide range of formal hardware memory models ( 𝗑𝟪𝟨 \mathsf{x86} , 𝖯𝖮𝖶𝖤𝖱 \mathsf{POWER} , 𝖠𝖱𝖬𝗏𝟩 \mathsf{ARMv7} , 𝖠𝖱𝖬𝗏𝟪 \mathsf{ARMv8} ) in the Coq proof assistant. Our proof is the first that establishes correctness of compilation of an event-structure-based model that forbids “out-of-thin-air” behaviors, as well as the first mechanized compilation proof of a weak memory model supporting sequentially consistent accesses to such a range of hardware platforms. Our compilation proof goes via the recent Intermediate Memory Model ( 𝖨𝖬𝖬 \mathsf{IMM} ), which we suitably extend with sequentially consistent accesses.

1
Introduction

A major research problem in concurrency semantics is to
develop a weak memory model that allows load-to-store reordering
(a.k.a.
load buffering
,
LB
)
and compiler optimizations (
e.g.,
elimination of fake dependencies),
while forbidding “out-of-thin-air” behaviors

[
19
,
11
,
5
,
14
]
.

The problem can be illustrated with the following two programs,
which access locations
x
and
y
initialized to
0
.
The annotated outcome
a=b=1
ought to be allowed for
LB-fake

because
1+a*0
can be optimized to
1
and then the instructions
of thread 1 executed out of order.
In contrast, it should be forbidden for
LB-data
,
since no optimizations are applicable.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}a\;{:=}\;[x]\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{~~\texttt{/\!\!/}\textit{1}}\\
[y]\;{:=}\;1+a*0\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}b\;{:=}\;[y]\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{~~\texttt{/\!\!/}\textit{1}}\\
[x]\;{:=}\;b\\
\end{array}\end{array}

(LB-fake)

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}a\;{:=}\;[x]\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}{~~\texttt{/\!\!/}\textit{1}}\\
[y]\;{:=}\;a\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}b\;{:=}\;[y]\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}{~~\texttt{/\!\!/}\textit{1}}\\
[x]\;{:=}\;b\\
\end{array}\end{array}

(LB-data)

Among the proposed models that correctly distinguish between these two programs
is the recent
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
model
[
6
]
.

\mathsf{Weakestmo}
was developed in response to certain limitations of earlier models,
such as the “promising semantics” of Kang
et al.

[
12
]
, namely that
(
i
) they did not cover the whole range of C/C++ concurrency features and that
(
ii
) they did not support the intended compilation schemes to hardware.

Being flexible in its design,
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
addresses the former point.
It supports all usual features of the C/C++11 model
[
3
]

and can easily be adapted to support any new concurrency features
that may be added in the future.
It does not, however, fully address the latter point.
Due to the difficulty of establishing correctness of the intended compilation schemes
to hardware architectures that permit load-store reordering (
i.e.,

\mathsf{POWER}
,
\mathsf{ARMv7}
,
\mathsf{ARMv8}
),
Chakraborty and Vafeiadis
[
6
]

only establish correctness of suboptimal schemes
that add (unnecessary) explicit fences to prevent load-store reordering.

In this paper, we address this major limitation of the
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
paper.
We establish in Coq correctness of the intended compilation schemes
to a wide range of hardware architectures that includes the major ones:

\mathsf{x86}
-
\mathsf{TSO}

[
18
]
,
\mathsf{POWER}

[
1
]
,

\mathsf{ARMv7}

[
1
]
,
\mathsf{ARMv8}

[
22
]
.
The compilation schemes, whose correctness we prove, do not require any fences
or fake dependencies for relaxed accesses.
Because of a technical limitation of our setup (see
Section

6
), however,
compilation of read-modify-write (RMW) accesses to
\mathsf{ARMv8}
uses a load-reserve/store-conditional loop
(similar to that of
\mathsf{ARMv7}
and
\mathsf{POWER}
) as opposed to the newly introduced
\mathsf{ARMv8}
instructions for certain kinds of RMWs.

The main challenge in this proof is to reconcile the
different ways in which hardware models and
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
allow load-store reordering.
Unlike most models at the programming language level, hardware models (such as

\mathsf{ARMv8}
) do not execute instructions in sequence; they instead keep track of
dependencies between instructions and ensure that no dependency cycles ever arise
in a single execution.
In contrast,
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
executes instructions in order, but simultaneously considers
multiple executions to justify an execution where a load reads a value
that indirectly depends upon a later store.
Technically, these multiple executions together form an
event structure
,
upon which
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
places various constraints.

\mathsf{IMM_{\mathsf{SC}}}
\mathsf{ARMv7}
\mathsf{POWER}
\mathsf{x86}
-
\mathsf{TSO}
\mathsf{ARMv8}
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
\mathsf{C11}

Figure 1
:
Results proved in this paper.

The high-level proof structure is shown in
Fig.

1
.
We reuse
\mathsf{IMM}
, an
intermediate memory model
,
introduced by Podkopaev
et al.

[
20
]

as an abstraction over all major existing hardware memory models.
To support
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
compilation,
we extend
\mathsf{IMM}
with
sequentially consistent
(SC) accesses
following the
\mathsf{RC11}
model
[
14
]
.
As
\mathsf{IMM}
is very much a hardware-like model (
e.g.,
it tracks dependencies),
the main result is compilation from
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
to
\mathsf{IMM}
(indicated by the bold arrow).
The other arrows in the figure are extensions of previous results
to account for SC accesses,
while double arrows indicate results for two compilation schemes.

The complexity of the proof is also evident from the size of the Coq development.
We have written about 30K lines of Coq definitions and proof scripts on top of
an existing infrastructure of about another 20K lines (defining
\mathsf{IMM}
, the
aforementioned hardware models and many lemmas about them).
As part of developing the proof, we also had to mechanize the
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
definition in Coq and to fix some minor deficiencies in the original definition,
which were revealed by our proof effort.

To the best of our knowledge,
our proof is the first proof of correctness of compilation of an event-structure-based memory model.
It is also the first mechanized compilation proof of a weak memory model
supporting sequentially consistent accesses to such a range of hardware architectures.
The latter, although fairly straightforward in our case,
has had a history of wrong compilation correctness arguments (see
[
14
]
for details).

Outline

We start with an informal overview of
\mathsf{IMM}
,
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
, and our compilation proof (
Section

2
).
We then present a fragment of
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
formally (
Section

3
)
and its compilation proof (
Section

4
).
Subsequently, we extend these results to cover SC accesses (
Section

5
),
discuss related work (
Section

6
) and conclude (
Section

7
).
The associated proof scripts and supplementary material for our paper
are publicly available at
http://plv.mpi-sws.org/weakestmoToImm/
.

2
Overview of the Compilation Correctness Proof

To get an idea about the
\mathsf{IMM}
and
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
memory models, consider a version of
the
LB-fake
and
LB-data
programs from
Section

1

with no dependency in thread 1:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}a\;{:=}\;[x]\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{~~\texttt{/\!\!/}\textit{1}}\\
[y]\;{:=}\;1\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}b\;{:=}\;[y]\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{~~\texttt{/\!\!/}\textit{1}}\\
[x]\;{:=}\;b\\
\end{array}\end{array}

(LB)

As we will see, the annotated outcome is allowed by both
\mathsf{IMM}
and
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
,
albeit in different ways.
The different treatment of load-store reordering affects the outcomes
of other programs.
For example,
\mathsf{IMM}
forbids the annotate outcome of
LB-fake

by treating it exactly as
LB-data
,
whereas
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
allows the outcome by treating
LB-fake
exactly as
LB
.

2.1
An Informal Introduction to
\mathsf{IMM}

\mathsf{IMM}
is a
declarative
(also called
axiomatic
) model identifying
a program’s semantics with a set of
execution graphs
, or just
executions
.
As an example,
Fig.

2(a)
contains
G_{\mathtt{LB}}
,
an
\mathsf{IMM}
execution graph of
LB

corresponding to an execution yielding the annotated behavior.

(b)

Execution of
LB-data
and
LB-fake
.

Figure 2
:
Executions of
LB
and
LB-data
/
LB-fake
with outcome
a=b=1
.

Vertices of execution graphs, called
events
,
represent memory accesses either due to the initialization of memory
or to the execution of program instructions.
Each event is labeled with the type of the access
(
e.g.,

{\mathtt{R}}
for reads,
{\mathtt{W}}
for writes),
the location accessed, and the value read or written.
Memory initialization consists of a set of events labeled

{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{0})
for each location
x
used in the program;
for conciseness, however, we depict the initialization events
as a single event with label
\mathsf{Init}
.

Edges of execution graphs represent different relations on events.
In
Fig.

2
, three different relations are depicted.
The
program order
relation (
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
) totally orders events
originated from the same thread according to their order in the program,
as well as the initialization event(s) before all other events.
The
reads-from
relation (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
) relates a write event
to the read events that read from it.
Finally, the
preserved program order
(
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
) is a subset of the
program order relating events that cannot be executed out of order.
Such
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
edges arise whenever there is a dependency chain between the
corresponding instructions (
e.g.,
a write storing the value read by a prior read).

Because of the syntactic nature of
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
,

\mathsf{IMM}
conflates the executions of
LB-data
and
LB-fake

leading to the outcome
a=b=1
(see
Fig.

2(b)
).
This choice is in line with hardware memory models;
it means, however, that
\mathsf{IMM}
is not suitable as a memory model for a programming language
(because, as argued in
Section

1
,

LB-fake
can be transformed to
LB
by an optimizing compiler).

The executions of a program are constructed in two steps.
1
1

1

For a detailed formal description of the graphs and
their construction process we refer the reader to

[
20
, §2.2]
.

First, a thread-local semantics determines the sequential executions of each thread,
where the values returned by each read access are chosen non-deterministically
(among the set of
all
possible values),
and the executions of different threads are combined into a single execution.
Then, the execution graphs are filtered by a
consistency predicate
,
which determines which executions are allowed (i.e., are
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent).
These
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent executions form the program’s semantics.

\mathsf{IMM}
-consistency checks three basic constraints:

Completeness:

Every read event reads from precisely
one write with the same location and value;

Coherence:

For each location
x
, there is a total ordering
of
x
-related events extending the program order so that each read of
x

reads from the most recent prior write according to that total order; and

Acyclic dependency:

There is no cycle consisting only of
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
edges.

The final constraint disallows executions in which an event
recursively depends upon itself, as this pattern can lead to
“out-of-thin-air” outcomes.
Specifically, the execution in
Fig.

2(b)
, which represents the
annotated behavior of
LB-fake
and
LB-data
, is
not

\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent because of the
({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}})
-cycle.
In contrast,
G_{\mathtt{LB}}
is
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent.

2.2
An Informal Introduction to
\mathsf{Weakestmo}

We move on to
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
,
which also defines the program’s semantics as a set of execution graphs.
However, they are constructed differently—extracted from a final
event structure
,
which
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
incrementally builds for a program.

An event structure represents multiple executions of a programs in a single graph.
Like execution graphs, event structures contain a set of events and several relations among them.
Like execution graphs, the
program order
(
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
) orders events according to each thread’s control flow.
However, unlike execution graphs,
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
is not necessarily total among the events of a given thread.
Events of the same thread that are not
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-ordered are said to be in
conflict
(
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}
) with one another,
and cannot belong to the same execution.
Such conflict events arise when two read events originate from the same read instruction
(
e.g.,
representing executions where the reads return different values).
Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}
“extends downwards”:
events that depend upon conflicting events (
i.e.,
have conflicting
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-predecessors)
are also in conflict with one other.
In pictures, we typically show only the
immediate conflict
edges
(between reads originating from the same instruction)
and omit the conflict edges between events
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-after immediately conflicting ones.

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{11}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{0})
{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}

(a)

S_{\mathsf{a}}

X_{\mathsf{b}}
\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{11}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{0})
e^{1}_{21}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}

(b)

S_{\mathsf{b}}
with execution
X_{\mathsf{b}}
selected

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{11}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{0})
e^{1}_{21}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}

(c)

S_{\mathsf{c}}

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{11}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{0})
e^{1}_{21}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}

(d)

S_{\mathsf{d}}
with execution
X_{\mathsf{d}}
selected

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{11}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{0})
e^{1}_{21}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{1}_{12}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
e^{2}_{1}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}

(e)

S_{\mathsf{e}}

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{11}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{0})
e^{1}_{21}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{1}_{12}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
e^{1}_{22}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}

(f)

S_{\mathsf{f}}
with execution
X_{\mathsf{f}}
selected

Figure 3
:
A run of
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
witnessing the annotated outcome of
LB
.

Event structures are constructed incrementally
starting from an event structure consisting only of the initialization events.
Then, events corresponding to the execution of program instructions are added one at a time.
We start by executing the first instruction of a program’s thread.
Then, we may execute the second instruction of the same thread or the first instruction of another thread,
and so on.

As an example,
Fig.

3
constructs an event structure for
LB
.

Fig.

3(a)
depicts the event structure
S_{\mathsf{a}}
obtained from the initial event structure by executing
a\;{:=}\;[x]
in
LB
’s thread 1.
As a result of the instruction execution, a read event
e^{1}_{11}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{0})
is added.

Whenever the event added is a read,
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
has to justify the returned value
from an appropriate write event.
In this case, there is only one write to
x
—the initialization write—and so

S_{\mathsf{a}}
has a
justified from
edge, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
, going to
e^{1}_{11}
in
S_{\mathsf{a}}
.
This is a requirement of
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
: each read event in an event structure
has to be justified from exactly one write event with the same value and location.
(This requirement is analogous to the
completeness
requirement in
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistency for execution graphs.)
Since events are added in program order and
read events are always justified from existing events in the event structure,

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
is guaranteed to be acyclic by construction.

The next three steps (
Figs.

3(b)
,
3(c)
and
3(d)
) simply add a new event to the event structure.
Notice that unlike
\mathsf{IMM}
executions,
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
event structures do not track syntactic dependencies,

e.g.,

S_{\mathsf{d}}
in
Fig.

3(d)
does not contain a
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
edge between
e^{2}_{1}
and
e^{2}_{2}
.
This is precisely what allows
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
to assign the same behavior to
LB
and
LB-fake
: they have exactly the same event structures.
As a programming-language-level memory model,
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
supports optimizations removing fake dependencies.

The next step (
Fig.

3(e)
) is more interesting because it showcases the key
distinction between event structures and execution graphs, namely that
event structures may contain more than one execution for each thread.
Specifically, the transition from
S_{\mathsf{d}}
to
S_{\mathsf{e}}
reruns the first instruction of thread 1
and adds a new event
e^{1}_{12}
justified from a different write event.
We say that this new event
conflicts
(
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}
) with
e^{1}_{11}

because they cannot both occur in a single execution.
Because of conflicts,
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
in event structures does not totally order all events of a thread;

e.g.,

e^{1}_{11}
and
e^{1}_{12}
are not
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-ordered in
S_{\mathsf{e}}
.
Two events of the same thread are conflicted precisely when they are not
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-ordered.

The final construction step (
Fig.

3(f)
) demonstrates another
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
feature.
Conflicting write events writing the same value to the same location
(
e.g.,

e^{1}_{21}
and
e^{1}_{22}
in
S_{\mathsf{f}}
)
may be declared
equal writes
,
i.e.,
connected by an equivalence relation
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
.
2
2

2

In this paper, we take
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
to be reflexive,
whereas it is is irreflexive in Chakraborty and Vafeiadis
[
6
]
.
Our
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
is the reflexive closure of the one in
[
6
]
.

The
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
relation is used to define
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
’s version of the reads-from relation,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
,
which relates a read to all (non-conflicted) writes
equal
to the write justifying the read.
For example,
e^{2}_{1}
reads from both
e^{1}_{21}
and
e^{1}_{22}
.

The
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
’s
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
relation is used for extraction of program executions.
An execution graph
G
is
extracted
from an event structure
S
denoted
S\rhd G

if
G
is a maximal conflict-free subset of
S
,
it contains only
visible
events (to be defined in
Section

3
),
and every read event in
G
reads from some write in
G
according to
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
.
Two execution graphs can be extracted from
S_{\mathsf{f}}
:

\{{\mathsf{Init},e^{1}_{11},e^{1}_{21},e^{2}_{1},e^{2}_{2}}\}
and

\{{\mathsf{Init},e^{1}_{12},e^{1}_{22},e^{2}_{1},e^{2}_{2}}\}

representing the outcomes
a=0\land b=1
and
a=b=1
respectively.

2.3
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
to
\mathsf{IMM}
Compilation: High-Level Proof Structure

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
e^{1}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}

(a)

TC_{{\mathsf{a}}}

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
e^{1}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}

(b)

TC_{{\mathsf{b}}}

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
e^{1}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}

(c)

TC_{{\mathsf{c}}}

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
e^{1}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}

(d)

TC_{{\mathsf{d}}}

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
e^{1}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}

(e)

TC_{{\mathsf{e}}}

\mathsf{Init}
e^{1}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
e^{1}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{1}:{{\mathtt{R}}}({y},{1})
e^{2}_{2}:{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}

(f)

TC_{{\mathsf{f}}}

Figure 4
:
Traversal configurations for
G_{\mathtt{LB}}
.

In this paper, we assume that
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
is defined for the same assembly language
as
\mathsf{IMM}
(see
[
20
, Fig. 2]
) extended with SC accesses and
refer to this language as
\mathsf{L}
.
Having that, we show the correctness of the
identity
mapping
as a compilation scheme from
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
to
\mathsf{IMM}
in the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\mathit{prog}
be a program in
\mathsf{L}
, and
G
be an
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent execution graph of
\mathit{prog}
.
Then there exists an event structure
S
of
\mathit{prog}
under
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
such that

S\rhd G
.

To prove the theorem, we must show that
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
may construct the needed event structure
in a step by step fashion.
If the
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent execution graph
G
contains no
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
cycles,
then the construction is completely straightforward:

G
itself is a
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
-consistent event structure (setting
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
to be just
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
),
and its events can be added in any order extending
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
.

The construction becomes tricky for
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent execution graphs,
such as
G_{\mathtt{LB}}
, that contain
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
cycles.
Due to the cycle(s),
G
cannot be directly constructed as a (conflict-free)

\mathsf{Weakestmo}
event structure.
We must instead construct a larger event structure
S
containing multiple executions,
one of which will be the desired graph
G
.
Roughly, for each
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
cycle in
G
, we have to construct an immediate conflict
in the event structure.

To generate the event structure
S
,
we rely on a basic property of
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent execution graphs
shown by Podkopaev
et al.

[
20
, §§6,7]
,
namely that execution graphs can be
traversed
in a certain order,

i.e.,
its events can be
issued
and
covered
in that order,
so that in the end all events are covered.
The traversal captures a possible execution order of the program
that yields the given execution.
In that execution order, events are not added according to program order,
but rather according to
preserved program order
(
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
)
in two steps.
Events are first issued when all their dependencies have been resolved,
and are later covered when all their
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-prior events have been covered.

In more detail, a traversal of an
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent execution graph
G
is a sequence of traversal steps between
traversal configurations
.
A traversal configuration
TC
of an execution graph
G
is a pair of sets of events,

{\langle{C,I}\rangle}
, called the
covered
and
issued
set respectively.
As an example,
Fig.

4
presents all six traversal configurations
of the execution graph
G_{\mathtt{LB}}
of
LB
from
Fig.

2(a)

except for the initial configuration.
The issued set is marked by
and the covered set by
.

A traversal might be seen as an execution of an abstract machine
that can execute write instructions early
but has to execute everything else in order.
The first option corresponds to issuing a write event,
and the second option to covering an event.
The traversal strategy has certain constraints.
To issue a write event, all external reads that it depends upon
must be resolved;
i.e.,
they must read from already issued events.
To cover an event, all its
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-predecessors must also be covered.
3
3

3

For readers familiar with
\mathsf{PS}

[
12
]
,
issuing a write event corresponds to promising a message,
and covering an event to normal execution of an instruction.

For example, in
Fig.

4
,
a traversal cannot issue
e^{2}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
before issuing
e^{1}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})

nor cover
e^{1}_{1}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
before issuing
e^{2}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
.

According to Podkopaev
et al.

[
20
, Prop. 6.5]
,
every
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent execution graph
G
has
a full traversal of the following form:

G\vdash TC_{\mathrm{init}}({G})\longrightarrow TC_{1}\longrightarrow TC_{2}\longrightarrow\ldots\longrightarrow TC_{\mathrm{final}}({G})

where the initial configuration,
TC_{\mathrm{init}}({G})\triangleq{\langle{G.\mathsf{Init},G.\mathsf{Init}}\rangle}
,
has issued and covered only
G
’s initial events
and the final configuration,
TC_{\mathrm{final}}({G})\triangleq{\langle{G.{\mathtt{E}},G.{\mathtt{W}}}\rangle}
,
has covered all
G
’s events and issued all its write events.

We construct the event structure
S
following a full traversal of
G
.
We define a simulation relation,
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,TC,S,X)
,
between the program
\mathit{prog}
,
the current traversal configuration
TC
of execution
G

and the current event structure’s state
{\langle{S,X}\rangle}
,
where
X
is a subset of events corresponding to a particular execution graph extracted from the event structure
S
.

Our simulation proof is divided into the following three lemmas,
which state that the initial states are simulated,
that simulation extends along traversal steps,
and that the similation of final states means
that
G
can be extracted from the generated event structure.

Lemma 2

(Simulation Start)
.

Let
\mathit{prog}
be a program of
\mathsf{L}
, and
G
be an
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent execution graph of
\mathit{prog}
.
Then
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,TC_{\mathrm{init}}({G}),S_{\rm init}(\mathit{prog}),S_{\rm init}(\mathit{prog}).{\mathtt{E}})
holds.

Lemma 3

(Weak Simulation Step)
.

If
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,TC,S,X)
and
G\vdash TC\longrightarrow TC^{\prime}
hold,
then there exist
S^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime}
such that

\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,TC^{\prime},S^{\prime},X^{\prime})
and
S\xrightarrow{}^{*}S^{\prime}
hold.

Lemma 4

(Simulation End)
.

If
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,TC_{\mathrm{final}}({G}),S,X)
holds,
then the execution graph associated with
X
is isomorphic to
G
.

The proof of
Theorem

1
then proceeds by induction on the length
of the traversal
G\vdash TC_{\mathrm{init}}({G})\longrightarrow^{*}TC_{\mathrm{final}}({G})
.

Lemma

2
serves as the base case,

Lemma

3
is the induction step
simulating each traversal step with a number of event structure
construction steps, and
Lemma

4
concludes the proof.

The proofs of
Lemmas

2
and
4
are technical
but fairly straightforward.
(We define
\mathcal{I}
in a way that makes these lemmas immediate.)
In contrast,
Lemma

3
is much more difficult to prove.
As we will see, simulating a traversal step sometimes requires us
to construct a new branch in the event structure,

i.e.,
to add multiple events (see
Section

4.3
).

2.4
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
to
\mathsf{IMM}
Compilation Correctness by Example

Before presenting any formal definitions, we conclude this overview section
by showcasing the construction used in the proof of
Lemma

3

on execution graph
G_{\mathtt{LB}}
in
Fig.

2(a)
following the traversal
of
Fig.

4
.
We have actually already seen the sequence of event structures constructed
in
Fig.

3
.
Note that, even though
Figs.

4
and
3
have the same number of steps,
there is no one-to-one correspondence between them as we explain below.

Consider the last event structure
S_{\mathsf{f}}
from
Fig.

3
.
A subset of its events
X_{\mathsf{f}}
marked by
,
which we call a
simulated execution
,
is a maximal conflict-free subset of
S_{\mathsf{f}}
and
all read events in
X_{\mathsf{f}}
read from some write in
X_{\mathsf{f}}

(
i.e.,
are justified from a write deemed “equal” to some write in
X_{\mathsf{f}}
).
Then, by definition,
X_{\mathsf{f}}
is extracted from
S_{\mathsf{f}}
.
Also, an execution graph induced by
X_{\mathsf{f}}
is isomorphic to
G_{\mathtt{LB}}
.
That is, construction of
S_{\mathsf{f}}
for
LB
shows that in
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
it is possible to observe the same behavior as
G_{\mathtt{LB}}
.
Now, we explain how we construct
S_{\mathsf{f}}
and choose
X_{\mathsf{f}}
.

During the simulation, we maintain the relation
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,TC,S,X)

connecting a program
\mathit{prog}
, its execution graph
G
,
its traversal configuration
TC
, an event structure
S
, and a subset of its events
X
.
Among other properties (presented in
Section

4.2
),
the relation states that all issued and covered events of
TC
have exact counterparts in
X
,
and that
X
can be extracted from
S
.

The initial event structure and
X_{\mathsf{\mathsf{Init}}}
consist of only initial events.
Then, following issuing of event
e^{1}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
in
TC_{{\mathsf{a}}}
(see
Fig.

4(a)
),
we need to add a branch to the event structure that has
{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
in it.
Since
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
requires adding events according to program order,
we first need to add a read event
corresponding to ‘
a\;{:=}\;[x]
’ of
LB
’s thread 1.
Each read event in an event structure has to be justified from somewhere.
In this case, the only write event to location
x
is the initial one.
That is, the added read event
e^{1}_{11}
is justified from it (see
Fig.

3(a)
).
In the general case, having more than one option, we would choose a ‘safe’ write event
for an added read event to be justified from,

i.e.,
the one which the corresponding branch is ‘aware’ of already and
being justified from which would not break consistency of the event structure.
After that, a write event
e^{1}_{21}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
can be added
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-after
e^{1}_{11}
(see
Fig.

3(b)
),
and
\mathcal{I}(\text{\ref{ex:LB}},G_{\mathtt{LB}},TC_{{\mathsf{a}}},S_{\mathsf{b}},X_{\mathsf{b}})
holds for

X_{\mathsf{b}}=\{{\mathsf{Init},e^{1}_{11},e^{1}_{21}}\}
.

Next, we need to simulate the second traversal step (see
Fig.

4(b)
), which issues
{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
.
As with the previous step, we first need to add a read event
related to the first read instruction of
LB
’s thread 2 (see
Fig.

3(c)
).
However, unlike the previous step, the added event
e^{2}_{1}
has to get value
1
, since
there is a dependency between instructions in thread 2.
As we mentioned earlier, the traversal strategy guarantees that

e^{1}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
is issued at the moment of issuing
e^{2}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({x},{1})
,
so there is the corresponding event in the event structure to justify the read event
e^{2}_{1}
from.
Now, the write event
e^{2}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
representing
e^{2}_{2}
can be added to
the event structure (see
Fig.

3(d)
)
and
\mathcal{I}(\text{\ref{ex:LB}},G_{\mathtt{LB}},TC_{{\mathsf{b}}},S_{\mathsf{d}},X_{\mathsf{d}})
holds for

X_{\mathsf{d}}=\{{\mathsf{Init},e^{1}_{11},e^{1}_{21},e^{2}_{1},e^{2}_{2}}\}
.

In the third traversal step (see
Fig.

4(c)
), the read event
e^{1}_{1}\colon{{\mathtt{R}}}({x},{1})
is covered.
To have a representative event for
e^{1}_{1}
in the event structure, we
add
e^{1}_{12}
(see
Fig.

3(e)
).
It is justified from
e^{2}_{2}
, which writes the needed value
1
.
Also,
e^{1}_{12}
represents an alternative to
e^{1}_{11}
execution of the first instruction of thread 1,
so the events are in conflict.

However, we cannot choose a simulated execution
X
related to
TC_{{\mathsf{c}}}
and
S_{\mathsf{e}}
by
the simulation relation since
X
has to contain
e^{1}_{12}
and
a representative for
e^{1}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})

(in
S_{\mathsf{e}}
it is represented by
e^{1}_{21}
)
while being conflict-free.
Thus, the event structure has to make one other step (see
Fig.

3(f)
)
and add the new event
e^{1}_{22}
to represent
e^{1}_{2}\colon{{\mathtt{W}}}({y},{1})
.
Now, the simulated execution contains everything needed,

X_{\mathsf{f}}=\{{\mathsf{Init},e^{1}_{12},e^{1}_{22},e^{2}_{1},e^{2}_{2}}\}
.

Since
X_{\mathsf{f}}
has to be extracted from
S_{\mathsf{f}}
,
every read event in
X
has to be connected via an
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
edge to
an event in
X
.
4
4

4

Actually, it is easy to show that there could be only one such event
since equal writes are in conflict and
X
is conflict-free.

To preserve the requirement, we connect the newly added event

e^{1}_{22}
and
e^{1}_{21}
via an
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
edge,

i.e.,
marking them to be equal writes.
5
5

5

Note that we could have left
e^{1}_{22}
without any outgoing

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
edges since the choice of equal writes for newly added events in
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
is non-deterministic. However, that would not preserve the simulation relation.

This induces an
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
edge between
e^{1}_{22}
and
e^{2}_{1}
.
That is,
\mathcal{I}(\text{\ref{ex:LB}},G_{\mathtt{LB}},TC_{{\mathsf{c}}},S_{\mathsf{f}},X_{\mathsf{f}})
holds.

To simulate the remaining traversal steps (
Figs.

4(d)
,
4(e)
and
4(f)
),
we do not need to modify
S_{\mathsf{f}}
because it already contains counterparts
for the newly covered events and, moreover,
the execution graph associated with
X_{\mathsf{f}}
is isomorphic to
G_{\mathtt{LB}}
.
That is, we just need to show that

\mathcal{I}(\text{\ref{ex:LB}},G_{\mathtt{LB}},TC_{{\mathsf{d}}},S_{\mathsf{f}},X_{\mathsf{f}})
,

\mathcal{I}(\text{\ref{ex:LB}},G_{\mathtt{LB}},TC_{{\mathsf{e}}},S_{\mathsf{f}},X_{\mathsf{f}})
,
and
\mathcal{I}(\text{\ref{ex:LB}},G_{\mathtt{LB}},TC_{{\mathsf{f}}},S_{\mathsf{f}},X_{\mathsf{f}})
hold.

3
Formal Definition of
\mathsf{Weakestmo}

In this section, we introduce the notation used in the rest of the paper
and define the
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
memory model. For simplicity, we present only a
minimal fragment of
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
containing only relaxed reads and writes.
For the definition of the full
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
model, we refer the readers to
Chakraborty and Vafeiadis
[
6
]
and to our
Coq development
[
17
]
.

Notation

Given relations
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, we write
R_{1}\mathbin{;}R_{2}
for their sequential composition.
Given relation
R
, we write
R^{?}
,
R^{+}
and
R^{*}
to denote its
reflexive, transitive and reflexive-transitive closures.
We write
\mathtt{id}
to denote the identity relation (
i.e.,

\mathtt{id}\triangleq\{{{\langle{x,x}\rangle}}\}
).
For a set
A
, we write
[A]
to denote the identity relation restricted to
A
(that is,
[A]\triangleq\{{{\langle{a,a}\rangle}\;|\;a\in A}\}
).
Hence, for instance, we may write
[A]\mathbin{;}R\mathbin{;}[B]
instead of
R\cap(A\times B)
.
We also write
[e]
to denote
[\{{e}\}]
if
e
is not a set.

Given a function
f\colon A\to B
, we denote by
{=}_{f}
the set of
f
-equivalent elements:
(
{=}_{f}\triangleq\{{{\langle{a,b}\rangle}\in A\times A\;|\;f(a)=f(b)}\}
).
In addition, given a relation
R
, we denote by
R|_{=f}

the restriction of
R
to
f
-equivalent elements
(
R|_{=f}\triangleq R\cap{=}_{f}
),
and by
R|_{\neq f}
be the restriction of
R
to non-
f
-equivalent elements
(
R|_{\neq f}\triangleq R\setminus{=}_{f}
).

3.1
Events, Threads and Labels

Events
,
e\in\mathsf{Event}
, and
thread identifiers
,
t\in\mathsf{Tid}
,
are represented by natural numbers.
We treat the thread with identifier
0
as the
initialization
thread.
We let
x\in\mathsf{Loc}
to range over
locations
,
and
v\in\mathsf{Val}
over
values
.

A label,
l\in\mathsf{Lab}
, takes one of the following forms:

•

{\mathtt{R}}(x,v)
— a read of value
v
from location
x
.

•

{\mathtt{W}}(x,v)
— a write of value
v
to location
x
.

Given a label
l
the functions
{\mathtt{typ}}
,
{\mathtt{loc}}
,
{\mathtt{val}}
return (when applicable) its type (
i.e.,

{\mathtt{R}}
or
{\mathtt{W}}
), location and value correspondingly. When a specific function assigning labels to events is clear from the context,
we abuse the notations
{\mathtt{R}}
and
{\mathtt{W}}
to denote the sets of all events labelled with the corresponding type.
We also use subscripts to further restrict this set to a specific location
(
e.g.,

{\mathtt{W}}_{x}
denotes the set of write events operating on location
x
.)

3.2
Event Structures

An
event structure

S
is a tuple

{\langle{{\mathtt{E}},{\mathtt{tid}},{\mathtt{lab}},{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}}\rangle}
where:

•

{\mathtt{E}}
is a set of events,
i.e.,

{\mathtt{E}}\subseteq\mathsf{Event}
.

•

{\mathtt{tid}}:{\mathtt{E}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tid}
is a function assigning a thread identifier to every event.
We treat events with the thread identifier equal to
0
as

initialization events
and denote them as
\mathsf{Init}
,
that is
\mathsf{Init}\triangleq\{{e\in{\mathtt{E}}\;|\;{\mathtt{tid}}(e)=0}\}
.

•

{\mathtt{lab}}:{\mathtt{E}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Lab}
is a function assigning a label to every event in
{\mathtt{E}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\subseteq{\mathtt{E}}\times{\mathtt{E}}
is a strict partial order on events,
called
program order
, that tracks their precedence in the control flow of the program.
Initialization events are
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-before all other events,
whereas non-initialization events can only be
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-before events from the same thread.

Not all events of a thread are necessarily ordered by
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
.
We call such
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-unordered non-initialization events of the same thread
conflicting
events.
The corresponding binary relation
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}
is defined as follows:

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}\triangleq([{\mathtt{E}}\setminus\mathsf{Init}]\mathbin{;}{=_{{\mathtt{tid}}}}\mathbin{;}[{\mathtt{E}}\setminus\mathsf{Init}])\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}^{-1})^{?}

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\subseteq[{\mathtt{E}}\cap{\mathtt{W}}]\mathbin{;}({=_{{\mathtt{loc}}}}\cap{=_{{\mathtt{val}}}})\mathbin{;}[{\mathtt{E}}\cap{\mathtt{R}}]
is the
justified from
relation,
which relates a write event to the reads it justifies.
We require that reads are not justified by conflicting writes
(
i.e.,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}=\emptyset}
)
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}^{-1}
be
functional

(
i.e.,
whenever
{\langle{w_{1},r}\rangle},{\langle{w_{2},r}\rangle}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
, then
w_{1}=w_{2}
).

We also define the notion of
external
justification:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\mathtt{e}\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
.
A read event is externally justified from a write if the write is
not
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-before the read.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\subseteq[{\mathtt{E}}\cap{\mathtt{W}}]\mathbin{;}({\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}\cap{=_{{\mathtt{loc}}}}\cap{=_{{\mathtt{val}}}})^{?}\mathbin{;}[{\mathtt{E}}\cap{\mathtt{W}}]
is
an equivalence relation called the
equal-writes
relation.
Equal writes have the same location and value,
and (unless identical) are in conflict with one another.

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}\subseteq[{\mathtt{E}}\cap{\mathtt{W}}]\mathbin{;}({=_{{\mathtt{loc}}}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}})\mathbin{;}[{\mathtt{E}}\cap{\mathtt{W}}]
is
the
coherence
order, a strict partial order that relates
non-equal write events with the same location.
We require that coherence be closed with respect to equal writes
(
i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\mathbin{;}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}\mathbin{;}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}
)
and total with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
on writes to the same location:

\forall x\in\mathsf{Loc}\ldotp~\forall w_{1},w_{2}\in{\mathtt{W}}_{x}\ldotp~{\langle{w_{1},w_{2}}\rangle}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}^{-1}

Given an event structure
S
, we use “dot notation” to refer to its components (
e.g.,

S.{\mathtt{E}}
,
S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
).
For a set
A
of events, we write
S.A
for the set
A\cap S.{\mathtt{E}}

(for instance,
S.{\mathtt{W}}_{x}=\{{e\in S.{\mathtt{E}}\;|\;{\mathtt{typ}}(S.{\mathtt{lab}}(e))={\mathtt{W}}\land{\mathtt{loc}}(S.{\mathtt{lab}}(e))=x}\}
).
Further, for
e\in S.{\mathtt{E}}
, we write
S.{\mathtt{typ}}(e)
to retrieve
{\mathtt{typ}}(S.{\mathtt{lab}}(e))
.
Similar notation is used for the functions
{\mathtt{loc}}
and
{\mathtt{val}}
. Given a set of thread identifiers
T
, we write
S.{\mathtt{thread}(T)}
to denote
the set of events belonging to one of the threads in
T
,

i.e.,

S.{\mathtt{thread}(T)}\triangleq\{{e\in S.{\mathtt{E}}\mid S.{\mathtt{tid}}(e)\in T}\}
.
When
T=\{{{\mathtt{thread}(t)}}\}
is a singleton, we often write
S.{\mathtt{thread}(t)}
instead of
S.{\mathtt{thread}(\{{t}\})}
.

We define the immediate
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}
edges of an event structure as follows:

S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po_{imm}}}\triangleq S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\setminus(S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}})\qquad\qquad S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf_{imm}}}\triangleq S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}\cap(S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po_{imm}}}^{-1}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po_{imm}}})

An event
e_{1}
is an immediate
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-predecessor of
e_{2}
if

e_{1}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-before
e_{2}
and there is no event
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-between them.
Two conflicting events are immediately conflicting if they have the same immediate
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-predecessor.
6
6

6

Our definition of immediate conflicts differs from that of

[
6
]
and is easier to work with.
The two definitions are equivalent if the set of initialization events is non-empty.

3.3
Event Structure Construction

Given a program
\mathit{prog}
, we construct its event structures operationally
in a way that guarantees completeness (
i.e.,
that every read is justified
from some write) and
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
acyclicity.
We start with an event structure containing only the initialization events
and add one event at a time following each thread’s semantics.

For the thread semantics,
we assume reductions of the form
{\sigma\xrightarrow{e}\sigma^{\prime}}

between thread states
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathtt{ThreadState}

and labeled by the event
e\in{\mathtt{E}}
generated by that execution step.
Given a thread
t
and a sequence of events
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\in S.{\mathtt{thread}(t)}

in immediate
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
succession (
i.e.,

{\langle{e_{i},e_{i+1}}\rangle}\in S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po_{imm}}}
for
1\leq i<n
)
starting from a first event of thread
t
(
i.e.,

\textit{dom}{({S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}};[e_{1}]})}\subseteq\mathsf{Init}
),
we can add an event
e

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-after that sequence of events provided that
there exist thread states
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}
and
\sigma^{\prime}
such that

\mathit{prog}(t)\xrightarrow{e_{1}}\sigma_{1}\xrightarrow{e_{2}}\sigma_{2}\cdots\xrightarrow{e_{n}}\sigma_{n}\xrightarrow{e}\sigma^{\prime}
,
where
\mathit{prog}(t)
is the initial thread state of thread
t
of the program
\mathit{prog}
.
By construction, this means that the newly added event
e
will be in conflict
with all other events of thread
t
besides
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
.

Further, when the new event
e
is a read event,
it has to be justified from an existing write event,
so as to ensure completeness and prevent “out-of-thin-air” values.
The write event is picked non-deterministically from all non-conflicting writes
with the same location as the new read event.
Similarly, when
e
is a write event, its position in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}
order should be chosen.
It can be done by either picking an
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
equivalence class and including the new write in it,
or by putting the new write immediately after some existing write in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}
order.
At each step, we also check for
event structure consistency

(to be defined in
Definition

5
):
If the event structure obtained after the addition of the new event is inconsistent,
it is discarded.

3.4
Event Structure Consistency

To define consistency, we first need a number of auxiliary definitions.
The
happens-before
order
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}
is a generalization of the program order.
Besides the program order edges, it includes certain
synchronization
edges
(captured by the
synchronizes with
relation,
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\mathtt{sw}}
).

S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}\triangleq(S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\mathtt{sw}})^{+}

For the fragment covered in this section, there are no synchronization edges
(
i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\mathtt{sw}}=\emptyset
), and so
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
coincide.
In the full model,
7
7

7

The full model is presented in
[
6
]
and also in our Coq development
[
17
]
.

however, certain justification edges (
e.g.,
between
release/acquire accesses) contribute to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\mathtt{sw}}
and hence to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}
.

The
extended conflict
relation
S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{ecf}}
extends the notion of conflicting events
to account for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}
;
two events are in extended conflict if they happen after conflicting events.

S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{ecf}}\triangleq(S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}^{-1})^{?}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}^{?}

As already mentioned in
Section

2
,
the
reads-from
relation,
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
, of a
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
event structure is derived.
It is defined as an extension of
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
to all
S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
-equivalent writes.

S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}\triangleq(S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}})\setminus S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}

Note that unlike
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}^{-1}
, the relation
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}^{-1}
is not functional.
This does not cause any problems, however, since all the writes from whence a read
reads have the same location and value and are in conflict with one another.

The relation
S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathtt{fr}}
, called
from-read
or
reads-before
,
places read events before subsequent writes.

S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathtt{fr}}\triangleq S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}^{-1}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}

The
extended coherence

S.{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathtt{eco}}
is a strict partial order
that orders events operating on the same location.
(It is almost total on accesses to a given location, except that it
does not order equal writes nor reads reading from the same write.)

S.{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathtt{eco}}\triangleq(S.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}\cup S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}\cup S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathtt{fr}})^{+}

We observe that in our model,
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathtt{eco}}
is equal to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}\mathbin{;}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}^{?}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathtt{fr}}\mathbin{;}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}^{?}
,
similar to the corresponding definitions about execution graphs in the literature.
8
8

8

This equivalence equivalence does not hold in the original
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
model

[
6
]
. To make the equivalence hold, we made
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
transitive,
and require
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\mathbin{;}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}\mathbin{;}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}
.

The last ingredient that we need for event structure consistency is the notion of
visible
events,
which will be used to constrain external justifications.
We define it in a few steps.
Let
e
be some event in
S
.
First, consider all write events used to externally justify
e
or one of its justification ancestors.
The relation
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\mathtt{e}\mathbin{;}(S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}})^{*}
defines this connection formally.
Among that set of write events restrict attention to those conflicting with
e
, and call that set
M
.
That is,
M\triangleq\textit{dom}{({S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}\cap(S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\mathtt{e}\mathbin{;}(S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}})^{*})\mathbin{;}[e]})}
.
Event
e
is
visible
if all writes in
M
have an equal write
that is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-related with
e
. Formally,
9
9

9

Note, that in
[
6
]
the definition of the visible events is slightly more verbose.
We proved in Coq
[
17
]
that our simpler definition is equivalent to the one given there.

S.{\mathtt{Vis}}\triangleq\{{e\in S.{\mathtt{E}}\mid S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}\cap(S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\mathtt{e}\mathbin{;}(S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}})^{*})\mathbin{;}[e]\subseteq S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\mathbin{;}(S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\cup S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}^{-1})^{?}}\}

Intuitively, visible events cannot depend on conflicting events: for every such
justification dependence, there ought to be an equal non-conflicting write.

Consistency
places a number of additional constraints on event structures.
First, it checks that there is no redundancy in the event structure:
immediate conflicts arise only because of read events justified from
non-equal writes.
Second, it extends the constraints about
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}
to the extended conflict
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{ecf}}
;
namely that no event can conflict with itself or be justified from a conflicting event.
Third, it checks that reads are justified either from events of the
same thread or from visible events of other threads.
Finally, it ensures
coherence
,

i.e.,
that executions restricted to accesses on a single location
do not have any weak behaviors.

Definition 5
.

An event structure
S
is said to be
consistent
if the following conditions hold.

•

\textit{dom}{({S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf_{imm}}}})}\subseteq S.{\mathtt{R}}

(
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf_{imm}}}
-read)

•

S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf_{imm}}}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}^{-1}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
is irreflexive.

(
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf_{imm}}}
-justification)

•

S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{ecf}}
is irreflexive.

(
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{ecf}}
-irreflexivity)

•

S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\cap S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{ecf}}=\emptyset

(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
-non-conflict)

•

\textit{dom}{({S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\mathtt{e}})}\subseteq S.{\mathtt{Vis}}

(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\mathtt{e}
-visible)

•

S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathtt{eco}}^{?}
is irreflexive.

(coherence)

3.5
Execution Extraction

The last part of
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
is the extraction of executions from an event structure.
An execution is essentially a conflict-free event structure.

Definition 6
.

An
execution graph

G
is a tuple

{\langle{{\mathtt{E}},{\mathtt{tid}},{\mathtt{lab}},{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}}\rangle}

where its components are defined similarly as in the case of an event structure with the following exceptions:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
is required to be total on the set of events from the same thread.
Thus, execution graphs have no conflicting events,
i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}=\emptyset
.

•

The
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
relation is given explicitly instead of being derived.
Also, there are no
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
relations.

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}
totally orders write events operating on the same location.

All derived relations are defined similarly as for event structures.
Next we show how to extract an execution graph from the event structure.

Definition 7
.

A set of events
X
is called
extracted from
S
if the following conditions are met:

•

X
is conflict-free,
i.e.,

[X]\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}\mathbin{;}[X]=\emptyset
.

•

X
is
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
-complete,
i.e.,

X\cap S.{\mathtt{R}}\subseteq\textit{codom}{({[X]\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}})}
.

•

X
contains only visible events of
S
,
i.e.,

X\subseteq S.{\mathtt{Vis}}
.

•

X
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}
-downward-closed,
i.e.,

\textit{dom}{({S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}\mathbin{;}[X]})}\subseteq X
.

Given an event structure
S
and extracted subset of its events
X
,
it is possible to associate with
X
an execution graph
G

simply by restricting the corresponding components of
S
to
X
:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\;}c@{\;}l@{\qquad}l@{\;}c@{\;}l@{\qquad}l@{\;}c@{\;}l}G.{\mathtt{E}}&=&X&G.{\mathtt{tid}}&=&S.{\mathtt{tid}}|_{X}&G.{\mathtt{lab}}&=&S.{\mathtt{lab}}|_{X}\\
G.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}&=&[X]\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\mathbin{;}[X]&G.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}&=&[X]\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}\mathbin{;}[X]&G.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}&=&[X]\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}\mathbin{;}[X]\\
\end{array}

We say that such execution graph
G
is
associated with

X

and that it is
extracted
from the event structure:
S\rhd G
.

\mathsf{Weakestmo}
additionally defines another consistency predicate to further
filter out some of the extracted execution graphs. In the
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
fragment
we consider, this additional consistency predicate is trivial—every extracted
execution satisfies it—and so we do not present it here.
In the full model, execution consistency checks atomicity of read-modify-write
instructions, and sequential consistency for SC accesses.

4
Compilation Proof for
\mathsf{Weakestmo}

In this section, we outline our correctness proof for the compilation from

\mathsf{Weakestmo}
to the various hardware models.
As already mentioned, our proof utilizes
\mathsf{IMM}

[
20
]
.
In the following, we briefly present
\mathsf{IMM}
for the fragment of the model
containing only relaxed reads and writes (
Section

4.1
), our simulation
relation (
Section

4.2
) for the compilation from
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
to
\mathsf{IMM}
,
and outline the argument as to why the simulation relation is preserved
(
Section

4.3
).
Mapping from
\mathsf{IMM}
to the hardware models has already been proved correct
by Podkopaev
et al.

[
20
]
, so we do not present
this part here.
Later, in
Section

5
, we will extend the
\mathsf{IMM}
mapping results to cover SC accesses.

As a further motivating example for this section consider yet another variant
of the load buffering program shown in
Fig.

5
.
As we will see, its annotated weak behavior is allowed by
\mathsf{IMM}
and also by

\mathsf{Weakestmo}
, albeit in a different way. The argument for constructing the

\mathsf{Weakestmo}
event structure that exhibits the weak behavior from the given

\mathsf{IMM}
execution graph is non-trivial.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}r_{1}\;{:=}\;[x]\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{~~\texttt{/\!\!/}\textit{1}}\\[2.84526pt]
[y]\;{:=}\;r_{1}\\[2.84526pt]
[z]\;{:=}\;1\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}r_{2}\;{:=}\;[y]\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{~~\texttt{/\!\!/}\textit{1}}\\[2.84526pt]
r_{3}\;{:=}\;[z]\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{~~\texttt{/\!\!/}\textit{1}}\\[2.84526pt]
[x]\;{:=}\;r_{3}\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 5
:
A variant of the load-buffering program (left)
and the
\mathsf{IMM}
graph
G
corresponding to its annotated weak behavior (right).

4.1
The Intermediate Memory Model
\mathsf{IMM}

In order to discuss the proof, we briefly present a simplified version of
the formal
\mathsf{IMM}
definition, where we have omitted constraints about RMW
accesses and fences.

Definition 8
.

An
\mathsf{IMM}
execution graph

G
is an execution graph (
Definition

6
) extended with one additional component:
the
preserved program order

{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}\subseteq[{\mathtt{R}}]\mathbin{;}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\mathbin{;}[{\mathtt{W}}]
.

Preserved program order edges correspond to syntactic dependencies guaranteed
to be preserved by all major hardware platforms.
For example, the execution graph in
Fig.

5
has two
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
edges
corresponding to the data dependencies via registers
r_{1}
and
r_{3}
.
(The full
\mathsf{IMM}
definition
[
20
]
distinguishes between
the different types of dependencies—control, data, adress–and includes
them as separate components of execution graphs. In the full model,
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}

is actually derived from the more basic dependencies.)

\mathsf{IMM}
-consistency checks completeness, coherence, and acyclicity:
10
10

10

Again, this is a simplified presentation for a fragment of the model.
We refer the reader to Podkopaev
et al.

[
20
]
for the full definition,
which further distinguishes between
internal and external
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
edges.

Definition 9
.

An
\mathsf{IMM}
execution graph
G
is
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent
if

•

\textit{codom}{({G.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}})}=G.{\mathtt{R}}
,
(completeness)

•

G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}\mathbin{;}G.{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathtt{eco}}^{?}
is irreflexive, and
(coherence)

•

G.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}\cup G.{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathtt{ppo}}}
is acyclic.
(no-thin-air)

As we can see, the execution graph
G
of
Fig.

5
is
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent
because every read of the graph reads from some write event
and, moreover, the
coherence
and
no-thin-air
properties hold.

4.2
Simulation Relation for
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
to
\mathsf{IMM}
Proof

In this section, we define the simulation relation
\mathcal{I}

11
11

11

A refined version of the simulation relation for the full
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
model can be found in
[
17
,
Appendix

A
]
,
which is used for the simulation of a traversal of an
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent execution graph
by a
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
event structure presented in
Section

2.3
.

The way we define
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,{\langle{C,I}\rangle},S,X)
induces a strong connection between events in
the execution graph
G
and the event structure
S
.
We make this connection explicit with the function
\mathtt{s2g}_{G,S}:S.{\mathtt{E}}\rightarrow G.{\mathtt{E}}
,
which maps events of the event structure
S
into the events of the execution graph
G
,
such that
e
and
\mathtt{s2g}_{G,S}(e)
belong to the same thread and have the same
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-position in the thread.
12
12

12

Here we assume existence and uniqueness of such a function.
In our Coq development
[
17
]
,
we have a different representation of execution graph events (but the same for events of event structures),
which makes the existence and uniqueness questions trivial.
More specifically, we follow Podkopaev
et al.

[
20
, §2.2]
.
There each non-initializing event
e
of an execution graph
G
is encoded as a pair
{\langle{t,n}\rangle}

where
t
is
e
’s thread and
n
is a serial number of
e
in thread
t
,
i.e.,
a position of
e
in
G.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
restricted to events of thread
t
;
each initializing event is encoded by the corresponding location—
{\langle{{\sf init}\;l}\rangle}
.
In this representation, the function
\mathtt{s2g}_{G,S}
for an event
e
returns (i) the
e
’s thread
and a number of non-initial events which
S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-preceded
e
if
e
is non-initialing
or (ii) its location if it is initializing:

\mathtt{s2g}_{G,S}(e)\triangleq\begin{cases}{\langle{S.{\mathtt{tid}}(e),|\textit{dom}{({[S.{\mathtt{E}}\setminus S.\mathsf{Init}];S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}};[e]})}|}\rangle}&\text{for }e\not\in S.\mathsf{Init}\\
{\langle{{\sf init}\;S.{\mathtt{loc}}(e)}\rangle}&\text{for }e\in S.\mathsf{Init}\end{cases}

Note that
\mathtt{s2g}_{G,S}
is defined for all events
e\in S.{\mathtt{E}}
,
meaning that the event structure
S
does not contain any redundant events
that do not correspond to events in the
\mathsf{IMM}
execution graph
G
.
The function
\mathtt{s2g}_{G,S}
, however, does not have to be injective:
in particular, events
e
and
e^{\prime}
that are in immediate conflict in
S

have the same
\mathtt{s2g}_{G,S}
-image in
G
.
In the rest of the paper, whenever
G
and
S
are clear from the context,
we omit the
G,S
subscript from
\mathtt{s2g}
.

In the context of a function
\mathtt{s2g}
(for some
G
and
S
),
we also use
{\llceil\cdot\rrceil}
and
{\llfloor\cdot\rrfloor}
to lift
\mathtt{s2g}
to sets and relations:

\displaystyle\text{for }A_{S}\subseteq S.{\mathtt{E}}

\displaystyle:{\llceil A_{S}\rrceil}\triangleq\{{\mathtt{s2g}(e)\mid e\in A_{S}}\}

\displaystyle\text{for }A_{G}\subseteq G.{\mathtt{E}}

\displaystyle:{\llfloor A_{G}\rrfloor}\triangleq\{{e\in S.{\mathtt{E}}\mid\mathtt{s2g}(e)\in A_{G}}\}

\displaystyle\text{for }R_{S}\subseteq S.{\mathtt{E}}\times S.{\mathtt{E}}

\displaystyle:{\llceil R_{S}\rrceil}\triangleq\{{{\langle{\mathtt{s2g}(e),\mathtt{s2g}(e^{\prime})}\rangle}\mid{\langle{e,e^{\prime}}\rangle}\in R_{S}}\}

\displaystyle\text{for }R_{G}\subseteq G.{\mathtt{E}}\times G.{\mathtt{E}}

\displaystyle:{\llfloor R_{G}\rrfloor}\triangleq\{{{\langle{e,e^{\prime}}\rangle}\in S.{\mathtt{E}}\times S.{\mathtt{E}}\mid{\langle{\mathtt{s2g}(e),\mathtt{s2g}(e^{\prime})}\rangle}\in R_{G}}\}

For example,
{\llfloor C\rrfloor}
denotes a subset of
S
’s events
whose
\mathtt{s2g}
-images are covered events in
G
,
and
{\llceil S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}\rrceil}
denotes a relation on events in
G

whose
\mathtt{s2g}
-preimages in
S
are related by
S.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
.

We define the relation
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,{\langle{C,I}\rangle},S,X)
to hold if the following conditions are met:

1.

G
is an
\mathsf{IMM}
-consistent execution of
\mathit{prog}
.

2.

S
is a
\mathsf{Weakestmo}
-consistent event structure of
\mathit{prog}
.

3.

X
is an extracted subset of
S
.

4.

S
and
X
corresponds precisely to all covered and issued events and their
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-predecessors:

•

{\llceil S.{\mathtt{E}}\rrceil}={\llceil X\rrceil}=C\cup\textit{dom}{({G.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}^{?}\mathbin{;}[I]})}

(Note that
C
is closed under
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-predecessors,
so
\textit{dom}{({G.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}^{?}\mathbin{;}[C]})}=C
.)

5.

Each
S
event has the same thread, type, modifier, and location
as its corresponding
G
event.
In addition, covered and issued events in
X
have the same value
as their corresponding ones in
G
.

(a)

\forall e\in S.{\mathtt{E}}.\;S.\{{{\mathtt{tid}},{\mathtt{typ}},{\mathtt{loc}},{\mathtt{mod}}}\}(e)=G.\{{{\mathtt{tid}},{\mathtt{typ}},{\mathtt{loc}},{\mathtt{mod}}}\}(\mathtt{s2g}(e))

(b)

\forall e\in X\cap{\llfloor C\cup I\rrfloor}\ldotp~S.{\mathtt{val}}(e)=G.{\mathtt{val}}(\mathtt{s2g}(e))

6.

Program order in
S
corresponds to program order in
G
:

•

{\llceil S.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}\rrceil}\subseteq G.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}

7.

Identity relation in
G
corresponds to identity or conflict relation in
S
:

•

{\llfloor\mathtt{id}\rrfloor}\subseteq S.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}^{?}

8.

Reads in
S
are justified by writes that have already been observed
by the corresponding events in
G
.
Moreover, covered events in
X
are justified by a write corresponding
to that read from the corresponding read in
G
:

(a)

{\llceil S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\rrceil}\subseteq G.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}^{?}\mathbin{;}G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{hb}}^{?}

(b)

{\llceil S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\mathbin{;}[X\cap{\llfloor C\rrfloor}]\rrceil}\subseteq G.{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}

9.

Every write event justifying some external read event
should be
S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
-equal to some issued write event in
X
:

•

\textit{dom}{({S.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}\mathtt{e}})}\subseteq\textit{dom}{({S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\mathbin{;}[X\cap{\llfloor I\rrfloor}]})}

10.

Equal writes in
S
correspond to the same write event in
G
:

•

{\llceil S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\rrceil}\subseteq\mathtt{id}

11.

Every non-trivial
S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}
equivalence class contains an issued write in
X
:

•

S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\subseteq(S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}\mathbin{;}[X\cap{\llfloor I\rrfloor}]\mathbin{;}S.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}})^{?}

12.

Coherence edges in
S
correspond to coherence or identity edges in
G
.
(We will explain in
Section

4.3
why a coherence edge in
S
might
correspond to an identity edge in
G
.)

•

{\llceil S.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}\rrceil}\subseteq G.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}^{?}

Figure 6
:
The execution graph
G
,
its traversal configuration
TC_{{\mathsf{a}}}
,
the related event structure
S_{\mathsf{a}}
,
and the selected execution
X_{\mathsf{a}}
.
Covered events are marked by

and issued ones by

.
Events belonging to the selected execution are marked by

.

As an example, consider the execution
G
from
Fig.

5
,
the traversal configuration
TC_{{\mathsf{a}}}\triangleq{\langle{\{{\mathsf{Init}}\},\{{\mathsf{Init},e^{1}_{3}}\}}\rangle}
,
and the event structure
S_{\mathsf{a}}
shown in
Fig.

6
.
We will show that
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,TC_{{\mathsf{a}}},S_{\mathsf{a}},X_{\mathsf{a}})
, where
X_{\mathsf{a}}\triangleq S_{\mathsf{a}}.{\mathtt{E}}
, holds.

Take

\mathtt{s2g}_{G,S_{\mathsf{a}}}=\{{\mathsf{Init}\mapsto\mathsf{Init},e^{1}_{11}\mapsto e^{1}_{1},e^{1}_{21}\mapsto e^{1}_{2},e^{1}_{31}\mapsto e^{1}_{3}}\}
.
Given that
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\mathtt{cf}}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}=\emptyset
, the consistency constraints hold immediately.
For example, condition
8
holds because
e^{1}_{11}
is
justified by
\mathsf{Init}
, which happens before it.
Finally, note that only
e^{1}_{31}
and
e^{1}_{3}

are required to have the same value by constraint
5
,
the other related thread events only need to have the same type and address.

The definition of the simulation relation
\mathcal{I}
renders the proofs of

Lemmas

2
and
4
straightforward.
Specifically, for
Lemma

2
,
the initial configuration
TC_{\mathrm{init}}({G})
containing only the initialization events
is simulated by the initial event structure
S_{\rm init}

as all the constraints are trivially satisfied
(
S_{\rm init}.{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}=S_{\rm init}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{jf}}=S_{\rm init}.{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\mathtt{ew}}=S_{\rm init}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}=\emptyset
).

For
Lemma

4
, since
TC_{\mathrm{final}}({G})
covers all events of
G
,
property
5
implies that
the labels of the events in
X
are equal to the corresponding events of
G
;
property
6
means that
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
is the same between them;
property
8
means that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathtt{rf}}
is the same between them;
properties
7
and
12
together mean that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathtt{co}}
is the same.
Therefore,
G
and the execution corresponding to
X
are isomorphic.

4.3
Simulation Step Proof Outline

We next outline the proof of
Lemma

3
,
which states that the simulation relation
\mathcal{I}
can be restored after a traversal step.

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,TC,S,X)
holds
for some
\mathit{prog}
,
G
,
TC
,
S
, and
X
, and we need to simulate a traversal step

TC\longrightarrow TC^{\prime}
that either covers or issues an event of thread
t
.
Then we need to produce an event structure
S^{\prime}
and a subset of its events
X^{\prime}

such that
\mathcal{I}(\mathit{prog},G,TC^{\prime},S^{\prime},X^{\prime})
holds.
Whenever thread
t
has any uncovered issued write events,

\mathsf{Weakestmo}
might need to take multiple steps from
S
to
S^{\prime}

so as to add any missing events
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\mathtt{po}}
-before the uncovered issued writes of thread
t
.
Borrowing the terminology of the “promising semantics”
[
12
]
,
we refer to these steps as constructing a certification branch for the issued write(s).
